ParlaMint-RS_2009-07-20-0.u24605	The word is made by MP Zoran Bortic.
ParlaMint-RS_2009-07-20-0.u24606	Dear President, the Honorable Presidency, respected ministers, representatives of the ministry, esteemed fellow MPs, despite the fact that there are more than 170 regulations in the Republic of Serbia that directly or indirectly refer to public health, which regulate the area of health care, health insurance, protection of the population from infectious diseases, etc., until now there was no law on public health, which regulates the area as a continent. For the adoption of such a law, there is also a constitutional basis in Article 97 of the 10th current Serbian Constitution, which found that the Republic of Serbia regulates and provides a system in the area of health. The health care law of Article 122(2) requires that the way and procedure, as well as the conditions for the organisation and implementation of public health, be governed by a special law. In addition to the above mentioned legal obligations, the adoption of public health laws is also necessary because of clearly defined areas and compliance with EU rules. The first steps to comply with EU public health regulations were made by adopting a Sustainable Development Strategy and a Public Health Strategy adopted on 17 March 2009. The public health strategy is to support the fulfillment of social care for health, to provide conditions in which people can be healthy. It forms the basis of measures taken by the government as part of actions to improve the health and quality of life of the population of the Republic of Serbia. This bill can be defined as a set of legal relations between rights and obligations, through which the state provides conditions for the health of the population and limits autonomy, privacy, ownership or other legally protected interests of individuals, with the aim of safeguarding and promoting health in general. This law regulates the achievement of public interest, the creation of conditions for the preservation and improvement of the health of the population, the preservation of the environment and the working environment, the prevention of the emergence and impact of risk factors for health, illness and injury, ways and procedures, as well as conditions for the organisation and implementation of public health. The law also defines certain concepts with a view to better understanding of the text, and public health is recognised as a realisation of public interest. The law also lays down the principles applicable to public health. It should be said that identifying public health functions is an ongoing topic around the world and that state and international health organisations are making huge efforts to improve their ability and seek to explicitly identify key problems. Thanks to this identification over the past decade, many countries have defined the basic functions of their public health system. As part of such objectives, the draft law regulates the social concern for public health at the level of the Republic, the AP and units of local self-government and divides and divides competences in the field of social care for public health. In this way, the principle of decentralisation has been halted in ensuring social care for health through respecting the specificities and needs of the population. The law also provides for cross-sectoral co-operation and guidance in the field of public health in a given territory. Ladies and gentlemen of the People's Parliament, within the framework of the health care system, the law on transfusiological activity is one of the most important, in my deep conviction, because this law regulates one of the most sensitive activities within this system. In fact, transfusiological activity has been embossed with the most specific form of health care and rests on a very high degree of volunteering, solidarity of people, which is giving their own blood to preserve the lives and health of others. That is why this area should indeed be regulated according to the world's highest democratic standards, because without defining the planning, collection, testing, conservation and distribution of blood, which is intended to transfuse the population, our country would not have the level of health and life that highly developed countries have, and especially EU countries. The adoption of this law aims to establish new more standards of health institutions that carry out transfusiological activity, but also to provide adequate amounts of safe blood and its components in our country. The aim is to ensure national self-satisfaction in providing safe blood and its components and high professional professional level and technological training to all complex transfusiological activity chains. Patients, as end-users, will receive standard conditions to provide them with quality and safe blood, i.e. the component of the blood collected, tested, produced, distributed and issued in accordance with the highest medical standards. The law introduces a single register of blood donors, which represents significant progress in legal regulation of transfusiological activity. Unfortunately, in my opinion, voluntary blood donors are completely neglected by this law, and we all know that they represent a pillar of the transfusiological system and that without their solidarity any effort in the area of security of precious fluid would be difficult to resolve. In other words, our country, without voluntary donors, would be forced to use blood or imports from abroad, or that the liquid that life means comes by some other commercial means, which would also match the basic principles of medical ethics. So I think this law had to valorize voluntary donors and make them a fundamental pillar in transfusiological activity, which they actually did. Otherwise, apart from this lack, I believe that this law in general is suitable for established standards in the modern international community. The adoption of organ transplant laws will put our country in line with highly organised states that establish normative and ethical standards in this, for many reasons, highly sensitive and controversial area of modern medicine. If there are any branches of medicine in which there can be inconspicuous consequences for human health, human safety, human life protection, then it's the area of human organ transplants. Therefore, the reasons for the adoption of the transplant law are more than convincing, since it systematically regulates the area of transplantation, i.e. the taking of organs or parts of organs from a living or dead person to transplant into the body of another person whose body is needed to preserve his life. The reason for the adoption of the law is also the need to carry out the organisation of health institutions carrying out transplant operations, to ensure supervision of the performance of these tasks, as well as to regulate other issues of importance for the organisation and transplantation of organs or parts of the oro. Only by law can standards be created which will be generalized to perform organ transplants in accordance with modern standards of medical science and practice. These standards are especially important because of the fact that the transplant is an area of medicine that develops intensively and offers great treatment opportunities and those diseases that are recently terminally incurable. The law will also create assumptions for promoting organ transplants at national and European level to inform and familiarise citizens with the importance of organ donation. By the way, the area of organ donation in our country is only immersed and this law creates real assumptions that the need for organ donation is developing among citizens to the extent that this consciousness has been developed in voluntary blood donors. I would like to point out that this law is in line with the European Union rules in this area, and that the area of tissue and cells is governed by a special law in line with the recommendation of the EU Commission to regulate the area of tissue and cell transplantation by a special law, independent of the organ transplant law. The law also introduces the institution of an ethnic committee in any health institution carrying out transplant operations. This Committee shall give its consent to the taking of an organ from a living donor on the basis of an insight into medical documentation, give an opinion on ethical and other issues in the transplant process and carry out other tasks under the proposal of the body transplant coordinator and at the level of the request of the ethnic biomedicine committee established in the biomedicine administration. The law accurately defines the process of transplanting and regulates the volunteering of the organ, the written consent of a full-time recipient, the written consent of a minor, or the recipient who has been deprived of the business capacity, the republican waiting list for the transplant of the development body for treatment, the prohibition of obtaining benefits from giving, receiving, or transplanting, the prohibition of advertising, or advertising, the ban on organ trafficking, the prevention of the risk of transmission of the disease, the financing of the transplant process, the monitoring of serious adverse reactions, as well as serious adverse events. The law also introduces an official secret, which introduces the use of data on the donor and recipient and their personalities, data storage and medical documentation, notification of the biomedicine administration and standard operating procedures and guides of good practice. The law also promotes voluntary organ donation and stipulates that the ministry responsible for health affairs is promoting voluntary organ donation and that the programme for the promotion is brought by the health minister. In general, the proposal for this law significantly norms this highly sensitive area of modern medicine and is in line with the modern EU rules governing this area. Ladies and gentlemen, MPs, in addition to the constitutional and legal basis for the adoption of the law on cell and tissue transplants, the proposal for the text that is before us is contained in a special law in line with the EU Commission's recommendations that the area of cell and tissue transplantation be regulated by a special law, independent of the organ transplant law. In a set of health care laws that we are discussing at this session of the Assembly, in my deep conviction, the most significant proposal for a law on the treatment of infertility is the procedures of biomedically assisted insemination. This law is significant for a number of reasons, and I will point to only a few of them. First of all, it is known that Serbia is plagued by the white plague and that we are becoming a country of old people and that with the average age we belong to the oldest nations in the world. Secondly, the social opportunities of the past decades, especially NATO bombings, the ecological and climate effects that war destruction has left on our country, have affected the alarming phenomena of total or partial sterility among our citizens of both sexes. Third, the transition crisis that we are going through, which results in a drastic poverty of a large number of people, social imperceptibility and uncertainty, and many other phenomena that destroy our families are increasingly leading to people in a fertilized age refraining from having children and trying to increase their family just at the end of that period. Unfortunately, they often face the terrible fact that in the meantime they have become completely or partially sterile. All of these phenomena bring Serbia into a position not only of ageing population but of total devastation and disappearance of entire settlements daily. These vicious facts create an Orvelovic perspective, on which our country, if it does not take energy measures, will soon be almost empty. As a result, and not only because of this, the draft law on the treatment of infertility by the biomedically assisted fertilisation procedures is a necessary effort by the state to take normative and all other measures to stop the systemic extinction of its population. In addition to this significant assumption of law-making, nothing is less important than the fact that this law, according to the highest European standards, will be sanctioned and all kinds of known and at this time unpredictable abuses and manipulations that follow this very sensitive branch of modern medicine. So that I don't owe, in the end, I want to wish the Ministry of Perseverance a lot of success in these laws and, as far as the SPS-JS Parliament Club is concerned, we will vote on these laws in the day of voting.

ParlaMint-RS_2009-07-20-0.u24611	Mr. Simo Vukovic has a word.
ParlaMint-RS_2009-07-20-0.u24612	Dear Mr Minister, the Honorable Presidents, ladies and gentlemen of the People's Parliament, we have before us a set of laws which, by their importance, fall within the rank of the law that this House passed back in 2005, such as the Health Protection Act and the Health Insurance Act. They represent the continuity of the normative reforms in health that began in 2002. In fact, the government of the Republic of Serbia and in general the Republic of Serbia has defined itself as a health advocate process in 2002 and to bring it into line with the EU strategy, and this includes harmonising norms and standards. The common denominator and value of these laws lies in that they will regulate vital areas of public health protection, which so far have either not been regulated or existing acts have been unarranged, anachrons and not in line with the latest standards. The adoption of the proposed laws is one of the steps in the process of Eurointegration, but it is often banalised, which we could hear from some previous discussions. However, it is a much more important element, and the bottom line is that by adopting and harmonising these acts with the EU's norm we actually adopt the most modern standards that are confirmed by medical science and practice. These laws introduce order in areas that regulate the responsibility of all actors in medical activities, as well as the obligation to apply the above-mentioned highest standards and good practice. When we talk about the law on public health, we must come from a common definition that has long and long been adopted by the World Health Organization, which reads – health is not only the absence of disease or incompetence but the state of total physical, mental and social well-being. It is precisely this conscious and ambitious setting of the definition that commits to a comprehensive social engagement in the promotion of preservation and improvement of the protection of the health of the population. We also need to note one important fact that, despite the great progress in the system and the sustainable financing of the protection system, for the past few years, there has been an expansion of so-called chronic non-infectious diseases consisting primarily of cardiovascular diseases, and then of different types of cancers, which are almost all directly related to inadequate prevention, and then exposure to risk factors and risky behaviour, as well as the unacceptableness of healthy lifestyles. With the current normative environment when we talk about a public health law that is the first in a series and represents one of the roof laws, the current normative environment has included, as the proposer also stated in his reasoning, over 170 regulations in various areas. Therefore, the need to adopt public health laws is more than obvious, to determine in one place both the tasks and the positions of what are different levels of responsibility and the legal relationship of all those actors who have responsibility in implementing public-health policy. So, what are the foundations of this law that existed practically since 2003 when the draft public health strategy was adopted, and then a strategy was adopted, as we heard this year in March, from which an appropriate action plan came out, so the agenda that he prescribed, that we will actually adopt and consider this year's public health law. It seems to me that public health as a concept and category are not fully understood for our wider public, so it seems to me to be part of the professional circles. Public health as a matter of activity, discipline, implies a precise definition of a public-health policy that regulates mechanisms, i.e. the responsibilities of all social actors, institutions and individuals in order to promote health, prevent diseases, strengthen awareness and institutions, their integration of which should be the result of preserving, improving the health of the population. The practice so far has shown that underdefined accountability allows for continued fingertips against Ministry representatives and the transfer of responsibilities to others, even though they existed in the previous period. This law clearly recognises the responsibility that, besides the Ministry and the Republic Insurance Office, as well as the Public Health Institute, responsibility will also belong to autonomous provinces, local self-governments, public enterprises, entrepreneurs, churches and religious communities, the Red Cross, different associations of citizens. The law is essential. It prescribes decentralisation of responsibility for health. This means the obligation of permanent education, public health and strengthening human resources, building a single information system, modernising and training of healthcare managers, setting up a sustainable system of financing activities and establishing standards for monitoring the quality and measurement of the results of the implemented public health policy. Another law I'm going to say about is the law on transfusiological activity, which regulates one of the vital areas in the health care system. The ultimate goal of this law is to provide self-sufficient amounts of safe blood, as well as its components. Starting with respect to these principles, therefore, the principles of self-satisfaction and blood security, the promoter has given the legal framework and space to clearly define who can deal with this activity, in which way, he has established a hierarchy of responsibility, which is necessary for one such action. In addition to the obligation to harmonise space with EU standards in this area, the proposed law obliges the application of the latest technologies, scientific knowledge and recommendations in the area of transfusions. The Authority of the State Administration that will propose regulations, criteria, standards and be a practical body that will establish this area in the way that the legislation is prescribed is a biomedicine administration within which there will be a special organisational unit that will deal with transfusion activity. At the top of the pyramid in terms of the breadth of activity and responsibility, there will certainly be an institution or the Republic Transfusion Institute. On the basis of the prescribed criteria, personnel equipment and other conditions, health institutions will be clearly authorised to deal with transfusiological activity. And not only that, the law also prescribes the formation of blood banks, which can exist in those health institutions that meet the prescribed criteria. It will clearly bisect the prescribed monitoring and control procedures of all phases from collection to distribution of blood and its components. In particular, this bill apostrophe of voluntary giving as a cornerstone of self-satisfaction in collecting. This law obliges permanent promotion and strengthening voluntary giving. The law also establishes the obligation to establish a single register of donors to run the Republic Transfusion Office. This registry will be part of a unique health information system, and it will allow for an insight into all the information that is relevant to the safety of blood. There is also a requirement to test each blood unit. It is important for the public to be aware of this, as well as of blood plasma testing, with even the use of the most modern pi-si-ar (PCR) technology. In particular, the obligation of certain health institutions to establish a quality management system in accordance with the most modern standards is prescribed. The obligation to apply a closed blood treatment system brings about the highest degree of safety, which is certainly in the interest of patients and citizens. What has often been the subject of media speculation and public fear is the existence of trade, or unauthorized exports of blood and blood derivatives. This law clearly prohibits imports and exports of blood and blood components in Article 40 and prescribes appropriate criminal provisions for offenders of this law. To conclude, the law on transfusiological activity can be seen as a very fundamental legal proposal, whose adoption and application is a need for better security and functioning of Serbia's health care system. The following three laws, the organ transplant law, the cell and tissue transplant law, as well as the law on the treatment of infertility in the procedures of biomedically assisted fertilisation are related laws, and I will try to unite the discussion concerning these three laws. Of course, all these three laws for the first time regulate the areas whose expansion everyone attended. We are the contemporaries of a technological and scientific boom that monitors the areas of both transplants and assisted reproductions. This technological and scientific progress in this period allows for the development of transplants and so on. artificial insemination to the extent that they have allowed in modern medical practice are actually today as routine methods of treatment. This did not go beyond Serbia, with the limited financial resources, and in particular the lack of legal regulation, creating a rather messy environment that does not contribute to patients' interests. These bill proposals, as well as these earlier ones, are regulated on the basis of the principles and standards that are present in the EU, with the aim of harmonising and applying the most modern standards of medical science and practice. First of all, the proposed laws will allow better promotion of organ transplants, which is very important, cells and tissues, meeting citizens with organ donations, cells and tissues, as well as reproductive cells. The laws that treat transplants, this is very important to highlight, are based on principles that have been widely accepted in the developed world. This is the principle of solidarity, the principle of medical justification for carrying out a particular procedure, the principle of protection of interest and dignity, both the donor and the recipient, the principle of availability and the prohibition of discrimination, as well as the principle of security. The proposed laws clearly define which institutions from a network of health institutions can deal with activities, which will be prescribed by appropriate criteria that must meet the institutions that want to run for such action. Through the establishment of the Biomedicine Administration, the Authority of the State Administration shall also establish the basis for the implementation of these laws and allow its professional potential to be implemented. In order to ensure the availability of the organs, as well as fairness in the transplant process, a single waiting list for the entire Republic of Serbia will be drawn up, according to the type of needs for organs and criteria that will be prescribed by the Biomedicine Administration. Many times mentioned by the Biomedicine Authority, it is certain that, during the course of time and adaptation of our entire legislative system, it will likely one day become an independent regulatory body, but the reality is that at this point, it is most optimistic that it is the authority of the state administration. We'll have three segments within this government body. It's a Serbian transplant, we're going to have an organisational unit dealing with organ transplants, cells and tissues and the third one I've already mentioned in the previous segment of the discussion dealing with transfusion activity. It is very important to establish Serbia's transplant within the Biomedicine Authority, because this will involve us in a broader context, in fact, a system that exists in Europe, which is Eurotransplant, which will allow the exchange of organs, and in particular to mean that if for a particular body with a deceased donor it is not possible to find a suitable recipient in our country and from a single waiting list, the body can offer an appropriate international organisation or institution, with which today's minister has established contractual co-operation previously. So we'll be able to exchange organs, cells and tissues based on reciprocity. This will mean that patients, once and for years waiting for the appropriate organ for transplants, can get to the organs much faster and be successfully cured. That wasn't legally regulated until today. The principle of non-profits should be mentioned, therefore, inconsistencies. Any form of profit above what covers the basic costs of the transplant or storage process is not allowed, therefore, any material or immaterial benefit in giving, receiving, or transplanting. It is explicitly forbidden to advertise or advertise the need for organs, cells and tissues, which is not related to the promotion of voluntary giving, which we need very much at the moment. The adoption of this law opens up a new space in which a much larger number of people will be given, if enough people are declared to be a voluntary donor, a corresponding donor card, on which an explicit consent to organ donation will be recorded. Based on the records of voluntary donors, a unique register of donors will be established at the Medical Administration. The data protection of both the donor and the organ recipient were also taken into account. The data will be treated as an official secret and will, to the greatest extent, protect dignity and prevent the possibility of abuse and possible legal disputes. Also, the proposed laws relating to the transplant also determine the way in which it is financed, and it is very important to point out, from the means of compulsory health insurance, but if there is no way and the right to be implemented, it can also be provided from voluntary health insurance. Also, organ, cell, and tissue donors, i.e. their health care, are provided by means of compulsory health insurance. These laws establish conditions for the formation of cell and tissue banks, banks of reproductive cells, as well as obligations and criteria for their deployment. This will also allow the availability of transplant biological material and the wider coverage of patient control by transplanting or biomedically assisted fertilisation. The law on the treatment of infertility through biomedical assisted fertilisation has specificity in relation to the previous two because it enters the domain of relations, above all, legal, and these are children's rights, the matter that regulates Family Law and the like, as well as certain ethical issues which one of the predecessors already launched in his discussion. It is clear what is meant by the procedures of biomedically assisted fertilisation and conditions when, how and who can deal with this activity. Also, the right to object to the conscience of a healthcare professional who participates in this procedure, if it is conflicted with ethical or religious beliefs, does not have to deal with this activity. The issue of paternity and motherhood in the birth of children in the biomedical assisted fertilisation process has also been addressed. It is stated that the motherhood, or fatherhood of the donor, cannot be established, which fully protects the treated couple, or parents from the future challenge of parenting. It should be noted for the public that virtually all jobs involving intervention on genetic material of reproductive cells or embryos are prohibited, including media often processed cloning. All five laws are made up of professional working groups and have passed a very serious public debate filter, so I am surprised that predecessors deny it, because practically the entire public concerned has been very well and openly informed about the public debates themselves, which seems to me to be a good guarantee of the validity of the proposed laws and I am convinced that their application will complete the legal framework for the best possible functioning of the health care system in Serbia. The Group of MPs and G17 Plus MPs will support these laws, because they are an expression of the continuity of the good reform policy of the Ministry of Health and we believe that they will appear to be the best interest of all patients and citizens in the Republic of Serbia.

In your explanation for the adoption of this law, you stated that it was necessary to harmonise domestic law in this area with EU law, and in this context, guidelines and recommendations by the EU and other international recognition of standards in the implementation of this action have been applied. In this bill, however, you have not consistently applied this, i.e. you have partially applied the European Commission Directives since 2002 and the daughter of the Directive since 2004 and 2005, and the recommendations of the Council of Europe, which set standards of quality and safety for the collection, testing, processing, storage and distribution of human blood, or blood components, in order to ensure a high level of protection of human health. We are also against the envisaged strategy of centralisation of transfusiological action. Serbia has co-ordinated a blood transfusion service with three regional centres and eight regional units. Three regional centres are not enough to adequately transport blood and blood components to remote areas. Given that it is impossible to standardise the quality of blood in the existing 47 places in Serbia where transfusiological activity is performed, adequate quality equipment could be provided, at least in those institutions where more units of blood are collected, say 500 and more. Therefore, local decentralisation could be done, i.e. the formation of a hospital blood transfusion service, as exists in some EU countries, Denmark, Italy, Sweden, given that five and a half million euros were spent on modernising transfusion activity. Not only has it not been done, but plasma derivative production has not increased, as promised. The principle of self-satisfaction, i.e. that each country has enough blood and blood components, and particularly self-satisfaction in plasma derivatives, plasma for francination, which are also EU guidelines, will be difficult to achieve. Reduced production not only in plasma derivatives, such as globulins and others, but also in blood collection systems from blood donors that the transfusion agency once produced and made a good profit. Economic reasons can always be an excuse that any stage in the process of blood collection and processing is not implemented, in which case there is no talk of self-satisfaction. Serbia achieves 10 per cent to 20 per cent confidence in the plasma for fractionation, while some EU countries and outside the EU accomplish far more. For example, Italy 60%, Spain 70%, Germany 80%, and 100% France, the Netherlands, Belgium, etc. Disrespect for voluntary blood donors and their associations as essential parts of civil society is anti-European. They were not involved in the task force for the making of this law. Giving blood is voluntary, anonymous and free, it is the personal act of every citizen and the awareness of it belongs to everyone. The problem, however, is that the legislator encourages people to volunteer for blood. We have intervened in this regard in Article 3, and we hope that the minister will accept the amendment. Therefore, there is no respect for the objections made by registered voluntary blood donors associations in the public debate. They are undervalued, which further threatens the trust of voluntary blood donors. The debate also mentioned the possibility of giving national pensions after a certain number of blood donations, but this already falls within the scope of other laws.
